one should please the tathāgatas.
oṃ vajra vāka rāga rāgaya hūṃ
I myself am action,
renowned throughout all directions.
I am also Gaganagañja;
worship intently with a rain of offerings.
oṃ vajra karma samaye pūjā hūṃ
I myself am protection,
the steadfast loving-kindness of the buddhas.
I shall worship the buddhas;
worship everywhere with the offering of Dharma.
oṃ vajra kavaca bandha rakā hūṃ
I shall devour the afflictions;
I am the steadfast Vajrayakṣa.
I shall worship the buddhas;
worship intently with the offering of Dharma.
oṃ vajra yakṣa māraya vajra daṃṣṭra hūṃ phaṭ
I am the awakening of the buddhas,
myself Vajramuṣṭi,
the wheel of nonregression;
worship intently with the offering of action.
oṃ vajra karma muṣṭi siddhya siddhya hūṃ phaṭ
All such various offerings
bring about the full attainment of the secret mantra of wisdom.
For all the goddesses and sattvas without exception,
as for the king, the heart is hūṃ.
Lāsyā, Mālā, and likewise Gītā,
Nṛtyā, Puṣpā and the rest: by applying them,
the sattvas and the other goddesses
are the offering of offerings, most carefully guarded.
Next, the rite that is the great means,
by which accomplishment comes swiftly,
by which one is joined to joy:
the ritual procedure of the fire offering.
Just as the fire offering, through its accomplishment,
performs unsurpassed rites,
all the other rites of knowledge mantras
do not act in such a way.
The fire offering has many kinds;
condensed in brief, it is of five kinds.
In detail, it is fully explained
in the secret sūtra of the Great Yoga.
The magical display of the knowledge holders,
how, through the kinds of fire offering,
the maṇḍala of the family is accomplished,
I shall explain in brief.
The rites of fire offering are of five kinds,
and each again has many kinds:
pacifying and increasing;
the third is the fierce rite;
summoning is the fourth;
the fifth is endowed with subjugation.
Of these fire offerings, the fifth,
subjugation, is supreme.
Next I shall explain the hearths;
they should be applied according to each application.
For pacifying the hearth is round,
perfectly round all around.
For increasing it is square;
for the fierce rite it is triangular.
A hearth in the shape of a vajra
is supreme for summoning everything.
Likewise, a hearth made long
is supreme for subjugation.

These are explained as five kinds;
the rites of the hearth are unsurpassed.
At dusk one should perform pacifying;
in the morning, the rite of increasing;
at midday one should surely perform
the violent rite of the fierce kind;
summoning at all times;
and at night, subjugation.
Through these five applications
one should perform the rites in meditative equipoise.
Facing east for the sake of pacifying;
for increasing, abide properly facing north;
having turned to face the south,
the fierce rites are performed.
Facing upward, likewise, for summoning;
subjugation in all directions.
Imagine all the intermediate directions joined equally;
or else it is explained as the western direction.
For pacifying, the buddha mudrā;
for increasing, the sign of the jewel;
Vajra Wrath for the fierce rite;
for subjugation, the lotus family;
endowed with powerful summoning,
Vajra Hook for summoning.
Through these five applications
one fully accomplishes the rites of fire offering.
The wood for pacifying is sweet;
for increasing, likewise, it bears fruit;
pungent for the fierce rites;
thorny for summoning;
for subjugation it is explained as bearing flowers.
These are the kindling sticks in their order.
First the yogin should properly arrange
the five kinds of kindling sticks.
Among them, wood that is smeared with mud
is declared to be dead.
Where evil is destroyed
and all turmoil is burned away:
with a fire of sweet firewood,
having kindled it in meditative equipoise,
make a fire offering of sesame oil,
and all evil will be burned.
oṃ sarva pāpaṃ dahana vajrāya svāhā
With sweet firewood
kindled in deep meditative equipoise,
having made a fire offering of rice,
the accumulation of merit will increase.
oṃ vajra puṣṭaye svāhā
With sweet firewood,
having kindled the fire in meditative equipoise,
through a fire offering of butter-soaked dūrvā grass,
vajra life will greatly increase.
oṃ vajra āyuṣe svāhā
With those same kindling sticks,
having kindled the fire as virtue arises,
with kuśa sprouts and sesame oil,
even the three worlds will be protected.
oṃ apratihata vajrāya svāhā
First one should apply pacifying,
and likewise increasing for the middle two;
the rites of fire offering
of pacifying and increasing come first.
With kindling of pungent wood
for the fierce rites,
and for summoning, together with the fierce rite,
the same applies as well.
With the samādhi of Vajra Wrath,
first, with pungent kindling,
having kindled the fire in wrath,
the learned one should make a fire offering of thorns.
And with wood that bears thorns,
with the thorny one certainly summons.
Whatever one may desire,
one certainly summons the buddhas.
oṃ vajra ākarṣaya jaḥ
With firewood, red flowers and the like,
having kindled a blazing fire,
if one makes a fire offering
of red flowers and fruits, it is supreme for subjugation.
hūṃ vajra pāśāya ja
Using pungent kindling sticks,
having kindled an extremely fierce fire,
if one burns bitter fruits,
it is supreme for the fierce rite.
hūṃ vajra māraya phaṭ
All the secret fire offerings
I shall next explain further.
Through the rite of the fire-offering wheel,
apply them there as is fitting.
With pungent kindling sticks,
having kindled the fire very fiercely,
make a fire offering with a terrifying hook,
and all the malevolent will be terrified.
oṃ vajra bandhaya phaṭ
In a fire of wood together with flowers,
with the samādhi of Vajra Wrath,
a fire offering of fruits, with mantra and the rest,
is supreme for subjugation.
oṃ vajra vaśaṃ karāya svāhā
Both bitter and sweet,
one should use sweet kindling sticks;
if one burns desirable flowers and fruits,
one becomes supreme, with whatever form one desires.
oṃ vajra karma rūpaṇe svāhā
Then, with those same firewoods,
having kindled the fire in great wrath,
burn the tiny seeds of bamboo,
and one swiftly becomes invisible.
oṃ vajra atriṣaya svāhā
With that same firewood of wisdom,
having kindled a fierce fire,
if one burns it with flowers of the power of the sky,
one will travel through the sky.
oṃ vajra khecarena svāhā
Next, messengers and servants,
those who do one's bidding, are urged on;
how they may be swiftly accomplished
I shall explain in order.
Bitter wood should be used as firewood;
dig the hearth in the shape of a vajra.
In wrath, burn vajra flowers,
and fully recite the syllable ja.
Through this application of mantra,
urge on the vajra mantra;
if one burns vajra flowers,
one will obtain the supreme servant.
oṃ vajra ājñā karāya ja
With those same kindling sticks,
one should dig that same hearth;
if one burns whatever medicinal substance belongs to the bodhisattva,
he will do one's bidding.
oṃ vajra sauri vaśaṃ kārāya ja
With a fire of those same firewoods,
in that very same hearth throughout,
if one burns the medicinal substance of Vajrasattva,
he will do one's bidding.
hūṃ vajra pāṇi vaśaṃ karāya ja
Or else the medicinal substance of the Buddha,
or else oneself and the signs:
make the fire offering through the application of accomplishment,
and the buddhas will do one's bidding.
oṃ buddha śaṃ karāya ja
Through this application of words,
perform the fire offering, and it is swiftly accomplished.
Through the vajra application all accomplishments
will be accomplished without doubt,
for all of them, the vajra family and the rest.
Next, the secret great seal:
in order to accomplish vajra yoga,
one should accomplish the great yoga.
Whatever consort is pleasing,
in order to obtain one of the sister's family,
together with a goddess consort
one should accomplish the yoga intensely.
Together with a lovely woman
the learned one should engage in dwelling together,
just as it is explained there
in the royal sūtra of the Lord of Secrets.
With saffron powder all over,
having inserted eye ointment into the bhaga,
one should know her sattva,
because the intention is thus proclaimed.
The consort of that beloved
is whoever is desired and pleasing;
whatever goddess is of one's own family,
she is known as "woman."
Through that union with the consort,
one accomplishes the deities of one's own family.
The inserting of eye ointment and the rest
means placing it properly upon the lotus.
bhaga is explained as "lotus,"
and inserting is to be explained as placing.
Through this application of the bhaga,
the eye ointments should be accomplished.
Inserting eye ointment into the bhaga
means placing it properly upon the lotus;
placing it together with the lotus,
the four kinds will be accomplished.
oṃ guhya rati vajra vaśaṃ karī siddha hūṃ
Warmth, smoke, blazing and dust,

the four kinds will be accomplished. oṃ guhya vajra siddha hūṃ Having placed bezoar in the bhaga, one should set down the secret seal. Through the four kinds of signs accomplishment is explained as fourfold. oṃ guhya vajri siddhi hūṃ With the seal of Vajrasattva, having inserted saffron into the bhaga, if, when it is covered, it blazes, the four kinds will be accomplished. oṃ vajra dhara guhya siddha hūṃ The application of vajra action, the condensed vajra seals, the absorption of Vajra Wrath, the application of Vajramuṣṭi, these supreme pledges, the finger application of hūṃ, likewise here the Dharma seal, the syllables beginning with oṃ, and through this rite of fire offering one should fully accomplish the wisdom seal. In detail, all of these are fully explained in the secret tantra. Through this application of the rite, the vajra master of great austerity will in this very life accomplish supreme buddhahood. In this delightful great vajra family, compiled from all the great tantras, whoever strives intently will gain unfailing accomplishment. For those who have not gathered the accumulations and have not seen the buddhas, this great secret essence will not even come to be heard. The rite of secret yoga is complete. Next, the four secret seals should be learned by one skilled in the maṇḍala. That very secret of wisdom, too, is fully turned once it has been accomplished. The great and utterly secret one should be placed so that it pervades all things. These are the four fierce vajras, together with oneself as the fifth: the supreme inexpressible secret, which brings attainment of the way of supreme wisdom, for the sake of fully accomplishing great bliss, great joy, utterly steadfast. oṃ sarva tathāgata vajra samaye hūṃ hūṃ vajra maṭa oṃ vajra prati bhṛkuṭī vajra rata oṃ vajra padma vajra tita oṃ vajra karma atri krodha In the eastern and southern quarters, in the western and northern quarters, in the center and the four directions, one should arrange the seals through application. The sublime maṇḍala of seals should all be drawn and arranged. Vajrasattva, the great king, will be seen; of this there is no doubt. In accordance with the yoga as explained, the subsequent yoga of the vajra arises. As for the remaining yogas, all of them should be made firm. The utterly secret is the great reality; the supremely secret is the supreme reality. The great secret holds the secret, the vajra secret, greatly steadfast. One who wishes for the knowledge mantra should draw it properly with his own hand. If one recites the great queen, accomplishment comes in the maṇḍala in an instant. oṃ sarva tathāgata viśuddha dharma hoḥ Having drawn the great maṇḍala oneself, one should strive in reciting the knowledge mantra. Through the application of summoning by loving-kindness, one should apply compassion to saṃsāra. oṃ viśuddha dheṣṭi jaḥ Having written the secret mantra oneself, and having kissed the girl's lips: the great queen of Lokeśvara, the goddess Prajñāpāramitā. oṃ svabhāva viśuddhaḥ mukhe hūṃ Through practicing the six perfections the conduct of awakening is very hard to find; but if the yoga of the Buddha becomes firm, one will accomplish it; of this there is no doubt. One should enter this maṇḍala, and with the five supreme sense pleasures one should sport and take delight. As explained in the sūtra of the Lord of Secrets, the full accomplishment of the five secrets is explained as the five sense pleasures. The tathāgata family and the rest are spoken of as the five sense pleasures. Through this application of desire the fortunate one attains accomplishment. That secret is the great reality: the full accomplishment of the five families. In order to fully accomplish great passion, one should sport and greatly delight. For the sake of accomplishing great bliss, the great conduct, the great way, the compendium of all the yogas of reality, I shall next properly explain. Through the yoga of Vajrahūṃkara one should accomplish the work of yoga. Just as, arising from the syllable hūṃ, it forms the great king of secrets, so through the application of Vajrahūṃkara one should accomplish the work of yoga. If it is not so, the great seal called the pledge will never be accomplished. Having condensed the great seal, fully take up the vajra seal. Next, the great seal of accomplishment, which through the true syllables of accomplishment brings swift and full accomplishment, the great marvel, I shall explain. oṃ sarva tathāgata vajra samaye hūṃ Abiding within the full disk of the Moon, fully take up the great seal; one should urge it on with the vajra mantra and fully accomplish the awakening of a buddha. hūṃ vajra maṭa As Vajra Vidyā, the supreme king, one should fully meditate on oneself; if one makes all the buddhas afraid, one will accomplish it; of this there is no doubt. oṃ bhṛkuṭī vartha Empowering oneself, fully take up the jewel seal; through raining down a shower of jewels one will accomplish it; of this there is no doubt. oṃ padma vajri hrīḥ th Oneself as the eye of wisdom, with the words, "One should urge it on with the vajra mantra," one should urge on the great lotus family. hūṃ karma vajra kriti ta The great king of the variegated vajra, the great self of various activities: if one worships all the buddhas, one attains the accomplishment of activity. Together with a lovely woman one should properly perform the maṇḍalas. From the royal sūtra of the Lord of Secrets the four kinds have come forth. Then, fully gathering the various characteristics, the one with wide eyes, supreme great joy, likewise the host, and she who has the vajra eye, with the eye of wisdom, endowed with the great supreme queen. oṃ vajra karma ho vajra hūṃ hūṃ kaṭa maṭa raṭa vaṭa karana vajrī gri vajra guru vajra phaṭ karara gri śuddhi ghi śuddhi śuddhaṃ gri buddha satva rāja rāga tuṣṭi ratna teja ketu hāsa dharma tīkṣṇa hetu ghoṣa dharma rakṣa yakṣa sanbhi siddhi yoga guhya viṣaka cārya cari jñāna ratva sumbha taga gruṃ hūṃ phaṭ hūṃ vaṃ hūṃ ca hūṃ ma hūṃ sa hūṃ ha hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ ja hūṃ ho hūṃ sa hūṃ aṃ hūṃ a hūṃ traṃ hūṃ taṃ hūṃ haṃ hūṃ dhaṃ hūṃ dhi hūṃ maṃ hūṃ raṃ hūṃ kaṃ hūṃ haṃ hūṃ vaṃ oṃ sarva tathāgata abhijñā oṃ sarva tathāgata cagresa oṃ sarva tathāgata viśāca oṃ sarva tathāgata cakre dhaṃ oṃ anudpanane a oṃ anuruhe ā oṃ anale i oṃ nīle ī oṃ ratne u oṃ ratta ū oṃ sa u ṛ oṃ la e ṝ oṃ ata ḷ

oṃ āhare ra ḹ oṃ āśane e oṃ bhehe ghe ai oṃ sarvāśā paripūrṇe o oṃ bharade au oṃ spharaṇe aṃ oṃ viśva aḥ oṃ mahoce bre ka oṃ tiṣṭha kha oṃ amukha ga oṃ abhiṣe gha oṃ jñāna ṅa oṃ ccheda ca oṃ vārāhirya cha oṃ bhaśe ja oṃ bhadhe jha oṃ praśame ña oṃ krodhi ṭa oṃ harane ṭha oṃ bhigha ḍa oṃ kṣave ḍha oṃ aṇu ṇa oṃ ṭakkile ta oṃ visule tha oṃ dhāraṇī da oṃ vatake dha oṃ valoke na oṃ trate pa oṃ sarva harani pha oṃ bhuśani ba oṃ bhibaśani bha oṃ arate ma oṃ alini ya oṃ traha ra oṃ viśa la oṃ buddhe va oṃ dhame śa oṃ yamabhe ṣa oṃ sarve sa oṃ dhalini ha ho tha hūṃ jaḥ hāsa oṃ a vaha aro nivi daśa traradhi bhupa saya ahi krodha dhamaśa bharaya yugati darśana hāsaya he mahāne dharaṇa oṃ sarva tathāgata savijñā mahāmune rājā garbho rājā kraya hi hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ ha This secret of the tathāgatas, since it is spoken in the sūtra of the Lord of Secrets, is of the steadfast nature of the dharmadhātu; it arises from the Dharma and from complete enjoyment. Nothing is equal to meditating on it for well-being. How wonderful! The great secret, the compendium of all secrets, the vast compendium of yoga, too, is without equal. This great bliss becomes steadfast, a steadfast reality. The master of great joy is the Sun of great joy; the compendium of the secret, worthy of worship, the compendium of all the maṇḍalas arising from the speech of the Omniscient One, has been spoken, by which the accumulations grow and greatly increase. The great vajra one, of great fortune, should train in all the secrets. The Dharma of reality, the great yoga: one should enter it in the dense compendium. One hundred and thirty-six and more, the compendium of all the maṇḍalas of the assembly: through drawing and the letters of the vajra words, one should train in the secret maṇḍala. The compendium of all the maṇḍalas of the tantra: into this secret maṇḍala, with the utterly pure letters of reality, one should enter the great yoga. It is square, with four gates, adorned with four archways, properly endowed with four lines, and adorned with silken tassels. Through the application of the Vajradhātu and the rest, the dense secret is utterly steadfast. In order to train in the secret of reality, the wise one should train in the maṇḍala. How wonderful! Through the application of the maṇḍala, the compendium of all the secret maṇḍalas, through the application of the Vajradhātu and the rest, the learned one should properly draw and arrange it. The tiers, equally square, should be made within it. In the central part of the maṇḍala one should place the form of the Buddha. In all its directions draw properly whatever is appropriate. On the maṇḍala whose nature is four-sided place thirty-six letters. In all the directions around the Buddha draw sixteen letters properly. The king of the sixteen letters, together with the host of seals and with light: on the four sides of the great maṇḍala draw the letters properly. Thirty letters, thirty each, should be placed in all the directions. In the center of the Buddha's heart one should apply the syllable hūṃ. The one who bears the name of the application should be placed in the center, as the Buddha. Fully applying the vajra letters, the wise one, as a holder of vajra knowledge, places the letters: in the center the vajra, and four vajras; in the east draw thirty letters; likewise thirty letters in the south; thirty in the northern quarter; and in the west one should apply thirty. Properly apply the sixteen letters; in the center make it endowed with four buddhas. Vajra yoga, great yoga, lord of yoga, great lord, glorious one of the world endowed with yoga, become the host of steadfast descent, great Victor arisen from speech: one should enter the yoga of the Buddha. uroti kota mahārya yodharya virte nāmane Then, through this application, one should set down the vajra wheel. ha ha ho ho, great secret, holding the trident and three locks of matted hair, great king, for the sake of great splendor: encircle it fully with vajra lines. Having looked upon it, the great master should fully recite the letters. Through the wheel of the vajra maṇḍala, emanate the vajra wheel everywhere. a ā: properly take up the yoga; i ī: the light of Maheśvara; u ū: the one who holds the sharp one; e ai: fully urging on the melodious voice; o au: the protector, the great spokes; aṃ aḥ: the excellent eye of wisdom. And with this Vajra Hook one should urge on Vajradhara. According to the wheel, according to the family, as is fitting, according to the vajra family, so one should fully arrange the wheel. The vajra master of great austerity and great fortune properly applies this secret vajra wheel and fully turns that magical display. Bringing down the wheel and setting it in place, one should enter according to the rite, just as it was taught at length in the maṇḍalas of the Vajradhātu and the rest. With the wheel of the maṇḍala of the family, endowed with the wheel maṇḍala, the place of the wheel comes under one's power, properly endowed with the wheel of Dharma. Vajra summoning, the fortunate one, the one who delights, the jewel, splendor, properly endowed with banner and laughter; Dharma, and the sharp one, the supreme chief; cause, and the fierce one, the great violent one; action, protection, the yakṣa, accomplishment: properly endowed with these various applications, the magical display of the wheel arises. Through such an application one should draw the vajra wheel all around. The master should bring all the vajra holders into this maṇḍala. To those who are not sons or disciples the great collection of secrets should not be given. Otherwise, to those endowed with fortune, you may give this text here. From the Vajra Peak, the great king of ritual of the supremely secret, utterly secret yoga, the king of the lords of the secret sūtras of all the tathāgatas, the explanation of the utterly secret reality of the vajra family, the fourth chapter, is complete. Then the Blessed One Vairocana, together with all the tathāgatas, once again supplicated Vajrasattva, the reality of all the tathāgatas, with these one hundred and eight names:

Vajrahūṃkara, supreme vajra,
holder of the supreme vajra, vajra arrow,
vajra frown of wrath, vajra body,
vajra blaze, great banner,
vajra peal of laughter, vajra lord,
vajra host, holder of the supreme Dharma,
Vajraketu, exceedingly sharp,
vajra wheel, great mantra recitation,
karma vajra, great vajra,
Vajrayakṣa, great terror,
vajra mudrā, great sound,
Vajramuṣṭi, great steadfastness,
vajra blaze, great vajra,
great action, great arrow,
Vajrasādhu, great joy,
vajra ornament that captivates,
vajra light, great banner,
vajra teeth, supremely joyful,
vajra lotus that utterly cuts,
vajra maṇḍala, vajra knowledge,
vajra tongue, great in variety,
great weapon of every kind, arrow for all creatures,
great armor, vajra body,
terrifying vajra fangs,
vajra binding, great vajra,
Vajra Wrath, great bow,
Vajra Hook, great treasury,
great noose, great power,
vajra chain, great in bringing down,
Vajra Descent, the terrifying,
the syllable jaḥ, the syllable hūṃ,
the syllable hūṃ that binds fast,
the syllable hoḥ, the syllable haḥ,
vajra mudrā, supreme in pride,
Vajrasattva, great wrathful one,
hook beyond all threat, vajra wrath,
vajra māra, supreme among māras,
utter destroyer of the māra of māras,
Vajra Joy, great peace,
vajra austerity, bringer of auspiciousness,
vajra fierce one, exceedingly savage,
vajra sovereign, maker of vajras,
vajra desire, great wrathful one,
vajra sound, great sound,
Buddha Hūṃkara, the syllable hūṃ,
equal to the syllable phaṭ and the rest,
the syllable phaṭ, the blazing command,
the syllable svāhā, without intrinsic nature,
lord who slays and who revives,
stainless one who terrifies and who brings relief,
buddha, great wrathful buddha,
wrathful vajra tathāgata,
suchness arisen from attachment:
Vajra Wrath, homage to you!

Whoever recites these sacred
one hundred and eight names
once each and every day
will attain the supreme accomplishment.
For them there will be no fear;
they will not become destitute or servants.
As taught by the Buddha Vajradhara,
they will attain the supreme accomplishment.

Hero, we supplicate you:
in order to tame the vicious
and likewise to benefit sentient beings,
please bring forth the great way.

The Great Yoga Tantra of the Secret called the Vajra Peak is complete.

It was translated and checked by the Indian preceptor, the great vajra master Karmavajra, and the translator monk Shönu Tsultrim.

The twelfth section.

Here is contained the Collection of Tantras, volume Ta.

Translator's notes

The supplicants' questions continue into the following piece; the closing quotation mark is placed before the section marker that ends this set. "Ritual procedure" renders རྟོག་པ (Skt. kalpa). The names Vajrarāja through Vajrarakṣa are the sixteen great bodhisattvas of the Vajradhātu maṇḍala; "the great fist" refers to the last of these, Vajramuṣṭi (also called Vajrasandhi). "Of which the great Lord of Secrets has spoken" reads the instrumental with གསུངས as a relative clause; it could also be taken as a request, "Great Lord of Secrets, speak!" "The supreme one born of the secret, and the secret of light rays" renders a terse line whose construction is uncertain. "Taken up" renders གདེག, referring to the dismantling or withdrawal of the maṇḍala at the end of the rite. The doubled syllable in ཇི་ལྟ་བུ་ཞིག་ལག་ལགས is treated as a copyist's repetition. "The six-faced one born of the host" renders གདུང་དྲུག་ཚོགས་བཙས, probably an epithet of Kārttikeya (Ṣaṇmukha), with གདུང read as གདོང ("face"); the identification is not certain. In the line "that I am their very self," ད་བདག་ཉིད is read as ང་བདག་ཉིད, ང ("I") fitting the following first-person verses. In "how does no fault arise," སྐྱེན is read as སྐྱོན ("fault"). In "lovely and steadfast," བསྟན is read as བརྟན. These verses voice the apparent contradictions of tantric teaching as questions; they are not instructions. The repeated phrase rendered "the compendium of all rites" (rtog pa thams cad bsdus pa) reflects Sanskrit kalpa in the sense of ritual system; the list appears to name the ritual cycles of the Yoga tantras (for example the Tattvasaṃgraha, Trailokyavijaya and Paramādya), but the titles are translated as this text gives them. "Ghana Akaniṣṭha" renders 'og min stug po; the later "sdug po" is read as the same place name (stug po). "bas gas pa" is read as bsags pa, "gathered," and "mtshungs bas" as mtshungs par. "rigs pas" before the snapping of fingers is read as rig pas, "with the vidyā." The line "Before and after there is no great bliss" and the phrase "the unmoving state" (g.yo ba med chos ma, the āsphānaka absorption) are uncertain in sense. The empowerment "of the cloth" (gos kyi dbang) likely refers to the bestowal of the silk diadem at the final stage of consecration. The closing section plays on the Tibetan rtog pa, which renders both kalpa, a ritual system or rite, and kalpanā, conceptual construction, alongside rtogs pa, realization. Dharmas are said to be free of conception, yet the teaching is called a rite; "rite" is used where the text defines its own term. "Dharma and enjoyment" likely alludes to the Dharma body and the enjoyment body. The ground of Perfect Joy is the first bodhisattva ground (Pramuditā). "Later tantra" renders rgyud phyi ma (uttaratantra); the text glosses each word in turn. In this piece drag po is used as an adjective, "fierce," of Vajradhara and his wrathful form, not as the name Rudra. "Compendium of All Secrets" renders gsang ba kun du bsdus pa, taken as the title of a cited source; it could also be read as "as taught in the gathering of all secrets." "The permanent, the Imperturbable, the Lord of Jewels, Amitābha, and the accomplishments" seems to name the five families by their lords (Vairocana, Akṣobhya, Ratnasambhava, Amitābha, Amoghasiddhi) in veiled form; rendered as the Tibetan stands. "Word from the wordless" reads tshig med for tshig me. "Most painful afflictions" renders shin tu sdug pa'i nyon mongs as written. The deities of this passage are the sixteen vajra bodhisattvas of the Vajradhātu maṇḍala, often named here by the second element only (Rāga, Sādhu, Hāsa and so on). "The four families" from which the occasion arises may refer to the four abodes of Brahmā named in the next line. In the line on the father and mother of the action family, "gnod sbyin srung ba" could name either Vajrayakṣa as protector or Yakṣa and Rakṣa together; it is read as one figure. The passage gives semantic etymologies of Sanskrit terms. "Joined with the prefix pra" renders rab sbyor, explaining prajñā as pra plus jñā; "na has the great meaning of negation" explains naya by its syllable na; "thinking" explains dhyāna from the root dhyai. The line on the yogin, shes rab kyi na des sems pa, is obscure as it stands; it has been read as "one who thinks by means of wisdom." The line "and I am the one who enjoys the five objects of desire" reads ལོངས་སྤྱོད་ཡི་ན as ལོངས་སྤྱོད་ཡིན. "Vajra jewels" (rdo rje rin chen) here describes the material of the palace rather than naming the deity Vajraratna. "Densely Arrayed" renders stug po'i zhing khams, the Ghanavyūha field. The Great Lord who answers the question on recitation is called dbang phyug chen po gsang ba, here rendered "Maheśvara, the Secret One"; in this tantra the epithet may well refer to the presiding Lord of Secrets rather than to Śiva. The colophon's phrase "from the sixth" (drug pa las) is translated literally; its reference is unclear. The last line, mchu yis ma yin gcig kyang min, continues into the next piece, which lists the other organs of speech not to be used; "nor with any one of them" is a provisional reading of gcig kyang min. "The mantra that joins the unjoined" renders sbyor med sbyor ba'i sngags literally. "The nature of reality" reads chos nyid for the written cho sa nyid, which makes no sense as it stands. "The goddesses" renders sman ma, here read as female deities; the line may instead intend medicinal plants. "Vajrakālī" renders rdo rje nag mo, "the black vajra goddess"; the identification is not certain. "The Vajra Eye" renders rdo rje spyan, possibly the deity Vajralocanā. The labels after the mantras are given as the text places them. "Until the accumulation is complete" renders ji srid tshogs bar du, whose exact sense is unclear. The phrase phyag rgya rgya rnams repeats a syllable and is read as "the mudrās." "Firm application to the Dharma" renders chos la nges sbyor. The total of eighteen comes from three for the buddha family, three for the vajra family, and four each for the jewel, lotus and action families. "The yoga of the abodes of Brahmā" (tshangs pa'i rnal 'byor) refers to the four immeasurables, the brahmavihāras. "One hundred and ten" renders brgya rtsa bcu as written. In the stanza on wisdom, the last line, sangs rgyas sems can dag gyur cig, could also be read "may buddhas and sentient beings become pure." "The supreme awakening," "the source of phenomena" and "śrīvatsa" are names of mudrās. The line on the Dharma, the enjoyment and the emanation seems to allude to the three bodies of a buddha. The figure called "the heart of the buddhas" (sangs rgyas snying po) in the action-family section may be a proper name (Buddhagarbha); it is rendered by meaning. In "do not bear the name of great elements" (byung ba che sgra 'dzin pa min) the sense is uncertain. "Indra gems" renders dbang po, probably indranīla (sapphire), though the identification is not certain. In "there is no such thing as form" the text reads bra ba, taken as a slip for bya ba; "de na" at the start of the action-family verses is taken as "de nas", "then". In the rosary section the Tibetan joins bo de tse to "sustaining life" (srog 'dzin pa), which may reflect a seed name such as putraṃjīva. "When, wearied by recitation, one rises" reads skyon ("fault") in a line one syllable short; it is taken as skyo ("weary"). "Should not be made smaller than five" reads chud for chung ("smaller"), an evident slip. The line on offering food (bza' bdag pa dbul bar bya) is unclear and is rendered simply as "offer the food". In the list of daily practices the text names three dharmas and the four called Brahmā's (the four immeasurables), yet says "in eight ways"; this is translated as it stands. The units for the maṇḍala sizes after "six cubits" (five, ten, six) are not stated and are presumably cubits. The line rendered "and you, having gone forth" (ci nas song ni khyed cag rnams) is obscure; it seems to address the disciples. "The secret eye" (gsang ba'i spyan) that the vajra mudrā summons is unclear. "The lying down" (nyal) probably refers to the sleep or night spent at the site during the preparatory days. The days counted (the fifteenth, the eighth, and so on) are days of the lunar month. "Foods to chew" and "foods to eat" read bca' bdag and bza' bdag, taken as bca' ba and bza' ba. "Vajrakrodha" renders rdo rje khro bo, "the vajra wrathful one", here paired with Vajrasattva as a deity to be saluted. The counts "a hundred and ten and more" and "eight kinds and ten more" follow the Tibetan (brgya dang rtsa bcu lhag; rnam pa brgyad dang rtsa bcu lhag), whose exact totals are unclear. The line "and not mantras and mudrās of that sort" is obscure in the Tibetan. "Prepare the food" reads bza' bdag, taken as bza' ba. The list of twenty fire offerings breaks off at the end of this piece. In the sexual yoga passage mtshan ma is the sexual organ, not "sign", and dbang po is the organ, not Indra. "Lord of the world" ('jig rten dbang phyug) is taken as an epithet of Vajrasattva, who is being addressed, though it could name Lokeśvara. In the western group of emblems rta babs ("portico") stands among animals and is taken as "horse". "Savor" renders bran par bya, whose sense is uncertain. "The great pigment" (tshon rtsi chen po) is translated literally; its esoteric referent is not explained here. "Supreme bliss" renders bde mchog, which here is a description of the accomplishment rather than the deity Cakrasaṃvara. The line on increasing life reads che 'phel, taken as a slip for tshe 'phel, "increasing life," as the accompanying mantra (āyuṣe) confirms. Pākṣa and śapuṣpa (sha pus pa) are offering substances that cannot be identified with confidence and are kept close to the Tibetan spelling. Under the syllable ho, tshags is read as chags, "passion." In "it brings attainment," thob ba is read as thob par. "Fire offerings of reality" renders de nyid, which echoes the title The Twenty Principles (de kho na nyi shu pa) given in the colophon line. "Supreme lord of unfailing purpose" renders don yod dbang phyug dam pa, taken here as an epithet of Vajrasattva rather than a separate deity. In the passage on the monk with shaven head, the Tibetan says only that long, coiled blue hair should be bound up, and the sense of the instruction is left as the text gives it. The placement of the four samaya deities around Vairocana is given tersely, and the lines naming them, including "the crusher of Māra" and "the vajra in the attitude of pride," are kept as they stand. "Vajra Desire, the great Māra" renders rdo rje 'dod chags bdud chen po, which may be an epithet within the Vajrasattva group rather than a separate figure. "The fourfold maṇḍala" could also be read as "the fourth maṇḍala." "Vajra tiriṃtiri" keeps the Tibetan sound-spelling of an implement that cannot be identified with confidence. The garbled spellings ད་གོད and མཆེ་བདག are read as dgod ("place") and mche ba dag ("fangs"). The frowning wrathful one (khro gnyer can) probably corresponds to Bhṛkuṭi, but the text gives no clear name, so the epithet is translated. The deities placed on the body of the all-formed central figure are identified as follows. ཐ་སྐར is read as the Aśvins, གཞོན་ནུ as Kumāra (Skanda), རྫོང as Durgā ("fortress"), ཚོགས་ཀྱི་དབང་ཕྱུག as Gaṇapati, and the flower-weaponed one as Kāma. "The Black One" and "Big Belly" translate མཆེ་བ་ནག་པོ and གསུས་པོ་ཆེ literally. ཉེ་བར་གདུང་བ is taken as the nāga Upatapta, with some doubt. རྗེ་ངར is unclear and is tentatively rendered "nape." "The Illuminator" renders སྣང་བྱེད, which follows Rāhu and Vemacitrin in a list of asura figures; its Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence, so it is translated by meaning. The line "Padma Sādhu are to be duly assigned" takes ལེགས་པ as the name Sādhu (the fourth attendant, after the sattva, hook and arrow), with སྦྱིན read as "assign"; it could also mean "bestow well". དྲ in པདྨའི་རིན་ཆེན་དྲ་སོགས is rendered "net" as written, but it may be a scribal slip; the next line lists the usual jewel-family attendants (Bhṛkuṭī, the Sun, the Banner). The closing བརྒྱ་དྲུག་ཅུ་རྩ་ལྔ་པའོ is probably a running count of sections or verses in the edition, and is translated literally. The names of the four secret goddesses around Vajrasattva and of the goddesses of the lotus and jewel families are translated by meaning, apart from Kāmeśvarī and Vajraśrī, because their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. The verse about these goddesses is hard to construe, and the reading given (her clasping the Blessed One's hand, another raising the makara banner) is a best attempt. "Lord of Jewels" renders རིན་ཆེན་དབང, apparently Ratnasambhava as the jewel-family buddha, but it is translated by meaning. The numbers one hundred and eighty-five and one hundred and eighty-nine probably count the deities of the maṇḍala. Several names of the outer-circle deities are translated by meaning (He of the Coiled Light, the Intoxicated One, the Escorts, the Silk-Clad One, the World-Renowned One, the chief who sports with the wheel) because their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence; the Silk-Clad One may be Indra under his epithet Kauśika. གཡུང་དྲུང་གཞོན་ནུ is taken as Sanatkumāra, "the eternal youth". "The ten thousand" in "those taught by the ten thousand" is literal, and its reference is unclear. The heading of the incense mantra reads འདུག་སྤོས, evidently a slip for བདུག་སྤོས, "incense", which the mantra itself (dhūpe) confirms; it is translated as "incense". The line about stepping on "the eyebrows" (སྨིན་མ) is translated as written; the sense of eyebrows in a list of maṇḍala features is unclear. "Hundreds of kinds and tenfold" renders སྣ་ཚོགས་བརྒྱ་དང་རྩེ་བཅུ, whose exact sense (literally "a hundred varieties and ten points") is unclear. The opening line in the request to the master, དགའ་ཆེན་ཁྱོད་བདག་སྟོན་པ་བས, is taken as an address, "Great Joy, you are my teacher". The piece ends in the middle of the disciple's request, which continues in the next piece. "The secret lord of tantras" renders རྒྱུད་དབང་གསང་བ, which may be the title of a specific tantra (compare the chapter colophons' "lord of secret sūtras"); it is translated descriptively. The jewel-family section lacks the closing line naming its family that the other sections have; it is translated as it stands. In the master's instructions the vehicles are "outer and secret," in the disciple's pledge "outer, inner and secret," as the text gives them. The phrase རྡོ་རྗེ་དྲིལ་བུ here clearly denotes the ritual implements vajra and bell, not the name used earlier, so it is translated as two objects. The final lines state that the great being should not examine whether the disciple is a vessel, which contrasts with the earlier instruction to examine; the following verse (that merely seeing the maṇḍala brings buddhahood) supports this reading. The opening lines (the continuation of the previous verse) are read as saying that even one who is not a vessel may be given mere admission, since seeing the maṇḍala alone is held to bring buddhahood; the full rites are reserved for those examined and found fit. The bare numbers after the descent mantra and the list of words with numbers (rite, ten; holding, twenty; and so on) are given as they stand; they appear to be recitation counts or a coded list, and their exact sense is uncertain. ཕེག་རྡོབ is taken as "clapping". Several verses in the section on the Supreme Secret (dissolution, threatening, the five suns, the star, binding, love and the three, the lowest of the highest) are highly condensed technical allusions; they are translated literally and their precise reference is uncertain. In the prose, ཕ་རོལ་པའི་འཁོར་ལོ is read as རོལ་པའི་འཁོར་ལོ, "wheel of play," as in the verse immediately after it. The last verse line about the king and the hundred queens of mudrā is ambiguous as to who or what becomes these. The opening section and the "host of mantras" consist of syllables with counts or numbers of applications; they are given as they stand, and their ritual purpose is not explained in the text. In ཀ་ཏནི the ནི is taken as a particle after the syllables ka and ta. The sixteen deities bound in pairs are the bodhisattvas of the Vajradhātu maṇḍala; their names are given in their standard Sanskrit forms (Vajraketu for the Vajra Banner). ཉེ་བར་བཞད་པ in the last line is taken as "gentle smile" rather than as the deity name Hāsa. The line "now, who is it that does not find a self?" is kept as the text's question; its point appears to be that on analysis anyone fails to find a self in the body of four elements. "Vajra descent" (རྡོ་རྗེ་དབབ་པ) is taken as introducing the following practices; its exact force as a heading is uncertain. This section reinterprets the gods of the Brahmanical pantheon (Prajāpati, Viṣṇu, the Sun, the Moon, Yama, Indra, Brahmā as the Grandfather, Bhava, Agni, Vāyu) and the cosmic levels (below the earth, upon the earth, above the earth, the three realms) as aspects of awakened mind; short labels such as "Indra:" and "Agni:" in the text name the god about to be explained. ལོ་ཆེན་འཇིག་རྟེན is taken as Mahāloka, one of the higher worlds of Purāṇic cosmology; this identification is uncertain. In the passage on the qualities, the lines saying one who attains the ground "does not gain conviction" and keeps to the Lesser Vehicle up to the eighth ground appear to describe a practitioner outside the secret path, in contrast with those who have entered it. This piece is a run of Maheśvara and other gods redefined in Buddhist terms, followed by a list of epithets of goddesses in the retinue. Most of these epithets cannot be matched with confidence to known Sanskrit names, so they are translated by meaning. བཟའ ("food") is read as གཟའ ("planets"), since the next lines turn to the grahas. The lines on "blessing" and the syllable tha seem to be an etymological wordplay that cannot be fully recovered from the Tibetan. ཕེག་རྡོབ is taken tentatively as a drum. In the closing section, "sign" (rtags) and "mark" (mtshan ma) stand for the male organ (liṅga), and the bhaga is the female organ; the text glosses these as wisdom and means. This is antinomian tantric verse that recasts killing, stealing, lying, desire and impure substances as figures for liberating realization; the text supplies its own glosses (for example, "wealth" as saṃsāra and "sentient being" as attachment to name and form). The text is translated literally, as it stands. The line "It is the family that degenerates" is ambiguous in the Tibetan; it may be a heading for the verses that follow. "The great rite" renders rtog pa chen po, echoing the title Compendium of All Rites (Sarvakalpasamuccaya). The verses follow the five aspects of complete awakening (pañcākārābhisambodhi) of the Yoga tantras: discerning one's own mind as a moon disk, the awakening mind as a second moon, the vajra, the vajra self, and the buddha body. "Lord of Unfailing Purpose" renders don yod dbang phyug, which cannot be confidently matched to a Sanskrit name. The line "formerly they brought all aims to accomplishment" seems to allude to Sarvārthasiddhi, the bodhisattva who awakens through these mantras in the Yoga tantra narrative. "The Densely Arrayed abode" renders stug po'i gnas (Ghanavyūha). The line listing the four kings, "Akṣobhya, Vajra, the Lord of Jewels, and the unsurpassed Amoghasiddhi," is translated as it stands; the western buddha does not appear by name in it. "Turning the wheel of Dharma" reads brtul ba as bskor ba, which the context requires. The "six intermediate directions" are translated as written. The verses link each of the sixteen great bodhisattvas to a virtue by wordplay on his name (hook, passion, Sādhu, jewel, splendor, banner, laughter, lotus, sharpness, wheel, speech, protection, fist). Without that background, some lines, such as "the being who is taught is unfailing," read obscurely, and they are translated as they stand. The line rendered "Scent turns away from nonvirtuous deeds" reads dring in the Tibetan, which makes no sense and leaves the line a syllable short; it is taken as dri ni, "scent". The list of sixteen secret names ends with Karmavajrī again, repeating the fourth name; this is translated as it stands. The goddesses called "Vajra Hammer Lady" and "Vajra Wisdom Lady" are translated literally because their Sanskrit names could not be identified with confidence. The couplet about the great-souled bodhisattva and the thirty-seven is obscure; it seems to mean that the four secret goddesses are not counted among the thirty-seven deities. Who is supplicated, and whose words Vajradhara hears, is also ambiguous in the verse: "the Protector" may refer to Vajradhara or to Vairocana. "The seven forms" of sexual intercourse likely refers to the seven kinds of sexual conduct (maithunasaṃyoga) which a celibate must give up, as listed in the sūtras. The couplet on the slight fault is terse; it seems to mean that one should not be careless of even a small fault, even at the cost of one's life. The lines about "those in the assembly who wish for the Lesser Vehicle" are obscure in the Tibetan ('khor pa rnam par gzhol ba); they are taken to mean that such hearers should be turned toward the Great Vehicle. "Bind with the vajra and the rest" renders rdo rje lta las sogs pa bcing, whose wording is unclear. "Vajra beak and great beak" translates rdo rje mchu and mchu chen po literally; these appear to be yellow substances whose identity is uncertain. Dri chu is taken as urine, used with mantra to consecrate the ground; it could also be read as scented water. The line "I am the supreme maṇḍala of the king" renders the Tibetan literally; it may mean that one asks to perform the supreme king of maṇḍalas. "Established in the fifth by means of the wheel" is rendered literally; the reference is presumably to a position or mudrā within the ritual sequence and is not specified in this piece. "Because one obtains the lofty secret" reads གང་ཕྱིན as the evident གང་ཕྱིར. The "Joyous ground" is Pramuditā, the first bodhisattva ground. "Vajra stone" for རྡོ་རྗེ་སྡོ reads སྡོ as རྡོ (stone); the reading is uncertain. The two lines "He holds its directions completely; he should be properly drawn and placed by the wise" repeat, in slightly altered form, the lines just before them and look like a copyist's dittography; they are translated as they stand. The secret names of the four goddesses are given in only three phrases, so it is unclear whether "Greatly Glorious" is a separate name; the names are translated literally. The four queens are Sattvavajrī, Dharmavajrī, Ratnavajrī and Karmavajrī, though the Tibetan writes the second and fourth as Vajradharma and Vajrakarma. "Sky Treasury" may stand for Gaganagañjā. The meaning of "split vajra" (rdo rje phyed pa) as an empowerment is uncertain. "For the great action, a sharp vajra" renders རྡོ་རྗེ་རྣོན་པོ་ལས་ཆེན་པོ literally; it presumably assigns the emblem of Vajrakarma, but the wording is unclear. "Armor" for Vajrarakṣa reads ཆོ་ག ("rite"), which makes no sense as an emblem, as the similar-sounding གོ་ཆ ("armor"), Vajrarakṣa's usual emblem. The emblems for the four corner goddesses are written rdo rje rin chen ("vajra jewel") and las ma ("action lady"), presumably meaning Ratnavajrī and Karmavajrī. "At the least" reads ཐ་མེ as ཐ་མ. "By the millions" renders བྱེ་བ; the line is obscure. "A hundred and seven more" renders བརྒྱ་ལས་རྩ་བདུན་ལྷག་པ literally; the reference, presumably to the number of signs, is unclear. The vajra master's request ("I, who am named such and such ... shall bring in the disciples") is in humble speech, addressed to the Sage. "If the samaya is impaired" reads མཉམ ("equal") as the evident ཉམས ("impaired, broken"). "This crushes your samaya" renders འདི་ནི་ཁྱོད་ཀྱི་དམ་ཚིག་འཇོམས literally; the sense is presumably that the oath water destroys one who breaks the samaya. "The lesser lines" renders དམན་མ, as in the earlier line about treading on the drawing; it could also mean "women", and the reading is uncertain. "Sixteen lifetimes" reads the evident ཚེ for the written ཚོ in གཞན་ཚོ་བཅུ་དྲུག. The lines "not so is it with trust in the other vehicles" and the four lines on the two countless eons and "sixteen" are terse and their sense uncertain; they seem to contrast the long path of the other vehicles with the sixteen births of one who has entered the secret maṇḍala, though the verse as it stands says "without entering the secret vehicle"; it is translated as written, and the line རྒྱལ་བའི་གནས་ཀྱིས་ཐོབ་ཅིང་ is a syllable short. ཕྲེང་བ is here plainly the flower garland cast onto the maṇḍala, not the goddess Mālā, so it is translated as "garland". "The Lord of Secret Sūtras" renders གསང་མདོ་དབང, apparently referring to the Tathāgata-guhya Sūtra Lord teaching named in the chapter colophons of this tantra. "The Jewel Lord" translates རིན་དབང literally; it presumably denotes the jewel-family lord (Ratnasambhava or Vajraratna), but this is not certain. The syllables ḍa ba ta are given as written; they appear to be a recited formula whose sense is unclear. The closing verses listing Sattvavajrī and the other goddesses are terse; they seem to name the four family consorts (of vajra, jewel, Dharma and action), but the exact grouping is uncertain. "First" reads སྔོན for the written སྡོན. "The one who keeps the vows" reads སྡོམ for the written ལྡོམ, which makes no sense here. The hymn in long lines interprets a series of seed syllables; many of its phrases are terse and their exact sense is uncertain, especially the glosses on the individual syllables. "The harmful ones" renders གནོད་མཛེས, taken as a variant of a term for harm-doers. "The ravishers" renders འཕྲོག་བྱེད literally; the class of beings meant is not certain. The first line reads smad byung, which makes no sense here; it is taken as the evident rmad byung, "marvelous," echoing the preceding line. "The Compendium of the Great Vehicle" renders theg pa chen po mngon bsdus, probably a reference to the Mahāyānābhisamaya, the title by which the Tattvasaṃgraha tradition designates its root scripture. "The unconquered" (ma pham) may alternatively be the epithet Ajita. Several verses of this section are terse and their syntax is open to more than one construal. "Whatever mind has meditated on the vajra" renders gang zhig as a relative ("whatever") because the clause is picked up by the predicate "is here called the subtle vajra." The two verses beginning "beginning with recollection at the crown" read rjes dran, "recollection," as written. "Bhūtas" renders 'byung po, which in this context of loving-kindness toward friends and non-friends may simply mean "beings" in general. "The two faculties" renders dbang po gnyis, which the text's own prose gloss here explains as the joining of one's own body with the body of the Buddha. "For a hundred and seven more" renders brgya las rtsa bdun lhag par literally; the sense may be a count of one hundred and seven (or more) repetitions. "The speech of all sentient beings: I am the sattva, Vajrabhāṣa" renders rdo rje smra ba, taken as the name Vajrabhāṣa; the preceding line reads sems dpa' smra ba brtan, "the steadfast speaking sattva," which may also be a further epithet of the same deity. The line "O yogins of the supreme secret" ends in the Tibetan with a stray syllable a, which is left untranslated. "First the intelligent one" reads sdon du as the evident sngon du, "first." "The Protector of the World" (Lokanātha) is taken here as an epithet of the buddha being worshiped rather than as the bodhisattva Avalokiteśvara. "Kha yi 'o" is read as "the kiss of the mouth" ('o being "kiss"). "Sign" renders mtshan ma throughout, which the text itself glosses as the hand emblem; in this sexual-yogic context it also carries the sense of the male organ (liṅga). In "Killing and desiccating", the Tibetan has gsang ("secret"), which makes no sense in this list of fierce rites; it is read as gsad ("killing"), a likely copyist's slip. The line on obstructors and merit in the fourth secret is terse, and its exact construction is uncertain. The line "If there were no mind of awakening" stands without its own conclusion in the Tibetan; the following lines ("Therefore this entire wheel arises from the mind of awakening") appear to supply it. "Nourisher" renders gso ba (Skt. poṣa), one of the standard synonyms for a self. The verb bsgral ba is here taken in its ordinary sense of delivering beings, not in its wrathful-rite sense. "The view of a perishable aggregate" renders 'jig tshogs (satkāyadṛṣṭi), the view of a real self in the aggregates. The instructions on the moons ("in the center of the moon, an equal moon"; "on that moon, two moons") are elliptical in the Tibetan and are rendered as they stand. The seed syllables and their deities follow the sixteen bodhisattvas of the Vajradhātu maṇḍala as this text pairs them; Vajranetra (rdo rje spyan) and Vajrarakṣa (rdo rje bsrung ba) are identified from the Tibetan. "Vajra essence" and "various vajra" are kept descriptive, as they may be epithets rather than names. The list of the six kinds of secret is terse, and the division into six is not explicit in the Tibetan. In "a sharp vajra" the Tibetan reads non po, taken as a slip for rnon po, "sharp." The verse beginning "Truth, become the supreme pledge" is elliptical, and its construction is uncertain. "Thirty million" renders "three koṭis." In "vajra mind, great vajra" the Tibetan rdo rje sems (elsewhere a short form of Vajrasattva) is taken as "vajra mind" because it stands in parallel with "mind of awakening" in the preceding line. In "one should recite the hundred and eight names", gdon is the verb "to recite", not the noun graha. The line rendered "with stainless enclosures" reads ra ba dag, literally "pure enclosures/fences"; the sense is uncertain. The name rendered Mahāsena reads dbung chen in the text; it is taken as dpung chen, "Great Army" (Mahāsena), a standard name of Kārttikeya. The names "the Invincible" and "Five Lights" (mi pham, 'od lnga) and "Human Lineage" (mi rgyud), given to the king who is Īśvara's ally, could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally. The name rendered "Fierce Vajra" (rdo rje drag po), the name Vajrasattva takes on as Vajrayakṣa, is translated literally because its Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. The consort named "the Terrifying Lady" (jigs byed ma) is translated literally since her Sanskrit name could not be fixed with confidence. In "Rudra and Brahmā have fled", drag po is taken as the name Rudra; it could also be an adverb, "fiercely". The text reads bsngags ("praised") where the context, and a later parallel line, require mngags ("dispatched"); the latter is translated. "Ten countless ten millions" reads bye bdag, a line one syllable short, evidently for bye ba dag. The verses of Maheśvara's reply, from "The vicious and the retinues of many buddhas" to "the words of my bhūtas," are syntactically obscure in the Tibetan and are rendered closely; their sense is uncertain. "A great din" reads sgru, evidently for sgra, "sound". The wrathful one from the navel is said to bear a hook (lcags kyu), although his mantra names the kīla; the text is translated as it stands. The deities here are named after the body parts or ornaments from which they emerge; where a Sanskrit name could not be securely identified, the Tibetan has been translated by meaning (Strength, Bracelet, Fist, Vajra Fingers, Vajra Claw Face, the Waist, the Crown, Vajra Limb, Austerity, the Foot, the Sole, Vajra Possession). The Tibetan for "Austerity" (dka' thub) stands where the mantra and the later list of names point to an anklet (nūpura); it is translated as written. "The Vajra Limb who draws in" renders rdo rje yi ni yan lag drangs, which the later list gives as "Fierce Limb" (yan lag drag); the reading is uncertain. In the list of secret names, gnod mdzes is taken as Sumbha (compare the vidyā that follows), and bdag cag ("we, us") is translated literally as "among us"; the line is obscure. "Devouring their own flesh" (rang gi sha za) could alternatively be read as "their own piśācas", but the context of wrathful fury favors the literal sense. Here mtshan means "names," not the major marks. "Vajrayakṣa the Crusher" renders rdo rje gnod sbyin 'joms; it answers the Great God's claim not to know Vajrayakṣa, and the mantra that follows is that of Nisumbha. In the Blessed One's question near the end, "this great being" is taken as Vajra Wrath, the agent who is exhausting Maheśvara's life. Maheśvara's line "Is that Vajradhara not cruel?" renders rdo rje 'dzin pa de gdug min, taken as a plea; it could also be read as a flat statement, "That Vajradhara is not malevolent." The buddha field Thal bas g.yogs pa and the tathāgata Thal ba'i dbang phyug sgra dbyangs can are identified with Bhasmacchanna and Bhasmeśvaranirghoṣa, the names Maheśvara receives in the parallel account of his subjugation. "Maheśvara and those with him" renders dbang phyug chen po bcas, which leaves unstated who is with him. "Vajra Tirinṭiri" is a transliterated name (rdo rje ti rinti ri) that could not be identified with confidence. Many of the vajra names and divine names in the lists are rendered by meaning where a Sanskrit form could not be securely identified (for example Vajra Coil, the Great Staff, the Honey Maker, the Leg Eater, the Crown of the Earth, the Vajra Eightyfold Lady, the Clothed One, the Noseless One). In "Piṅgala Vajra Piṅgala" the Tibetan ends in min ("not"), most likely a slip for mig, and is rendered by context. In the line on the Terrifier, the syllables nga na are obscure and have not been given a separate rendering. The line "Lest your life be ruined, likewise do not give the samaya away" is a tentative reading of an elliptical couplet. "These are words to be interpreted" renders 'di ni gzhug pa'i tshig yin; the verses that follow explain the burning figuratively, as the burning of the afflictions with the fire of wisdom. The piśācas' line "Let us do just as he says; yet we need not speak the truth" is taken as their plotting deceit, which fits Vajradhara's renewed wrath; it could also be read as an instruction not to speak truthfully. "With my blazing right hand" renders g.yas 'bar bas, which is terse. The syllable ཤ standing between the mantra "oṃ kṣepaya vajra svāhā" and the following seven-syllable line "rdo rje sems dpa'i thabs chen po" fits neither and appears to be a stray syllable; it is left untranslated. In "the great multicolored being" the text reads ཁྲ་བོ, rendered per the house glossary; it may be a variant for ཁྲོ་བོ, the wrathful one emanated just before. "The King of the Crown" renders སྤྱི་གཙུག་རྒྱལ་པོ, apparently an Uṣṇīṣa wrathful king; the Sanskrit form is not certain. "No stars" reads སྐུར་མ as a slip for སྐར་མ (stars), which fits beside sun and moon. The sense of ཁ་ཁྱེར ("opened out") in "the outer abodes appeared opened out" is uncertain. ཁྲོ་བོ་དྲུག་བདུན is taken as "six or seven wrathful ones"; it could conceivably mean sixty-seven. ལོག in "thunder and great lightning" is read as a slip for གློག (lightning). In the color sequence the north is given as བཙག / བཙོད (rendered "ochre" and "madder") and likened to vaiḍūrya; in the five-family scheme this quarter is normally green, and the terms may stand for that color here. སྡོན་པོ in "blue vajras" is read as སྔོན་པོ. ཁ་ཁྱེར, used three times of the maṇḍala's architecture, is rendered "borders"; its precise sense is uncertain. བཀོད་དཀའ in "a garland of flames unbearable" is read as བཟོད་དཀའ (hard to bear), as in the parallel line that follows. The couplet "the ten sons of the Great Fierce One, and then delightful things, arose" is obscure, and the identity of the Great Fierce One (དྲག་པོ་ཆེན་པོ) is not stated. "Always delighting in mirth" renders the obscure རྣམ་པར་ད་གོད་ལ་དགའ, taking ད་གོད as a form of དགོད (laughter); this is uncertain. རྟ་བབས་བཞེས is read as རྟ་བབས་བཞི (four porticoes). "With his right foot presses down" supplies "foot" for the bare "with the right" (གཡས་པས), as the trampling posture requires. The wrathful attendants of the south (Wrathful Radiance, Fierce Banner, Wrathful Laughter) correspond to Vajratejas, Vajraketu and Vajrahāsa of the Vajradhātu maṇḍala. "The great Vajra Descent of action" renders rdo rje dbab pa las chen po, the principal of the fourth (northern) maṇḍala; "descent" (āveśa) is the ritual descent or entering of the deity, and the exact Sanskrit name of this figure is uncertain. The "enclosure" (kha khyer) is the outer precinct of the maṇḍala where the worldly deities are placed. "The two organs" renders dbang po gnyis (two indriyas), here referring to the sexual union of deity and consort, which the closing prose reinterprets as the knowledge of the equality of the deity's body and one's own. "Made to receive the descent" renders dbab pa, the ritual descent (āveśa) of the deity or of wisdom into the participant. The lines on "the hardships of countless eons" are terse; the sense appears to be that the long path of hardship has its own practice, while for one diligent in this yoga the grounds are easily won. The "Compendium of the Great Vehicle" (theg pa chen po mngon bsdus) is taken as the title of a source text, possibly the Tattvasaṃgraha; this is uncertain. "The ground of Great Joy" is the first bodhisattva ground (Pramuditā). Vajratejas, Vajraketu, Vajrahāsa and "Vajra Armor" (Vajrakavaca) are identified by their standard Vajradhātu roles and the accompanying seed words. This piece is terse, with many compressed verse lines; the sequence of buddhas and their maṇḍalas (Ratnasambhava's guide setting out Vajra Joy, followed by the eastern maṇḍala of Akṣobhya) is rendered as the text stands. "The Vajra Bewilderer" renders rdo rje rmongs byed ma (probably Vajramohanī) and "the Vajra Queen" renders rdo rje rgyal mo; their Sanskrit forms are not certain. "Sisters" (sring mo) are the female attendant deities of the retinue. "The meteoric iron" (gnam lcags) and "summoning strokes" (pheg rdob) in the list of gate deities and offerings are terse and their exact referents uncertain. "One becomes rigid" renders reng par 'gyur as written, though its sense in connection with Vajrarāja is unclear. "The ground" in "the rites for protecting the ground" is taken as the ritual site. "The Supreme Secret is complete" marks the end of a section. The second Vajrarakṣa stanza is followed by a Vajrayakṣa mantra, but the Tibetan names Vajrarakṣa (rdo rje srung ba) again, so it is translated as written. In the Buddha stanza the text reads pham ("defeat"), evidently a slip for phab ("bring down"), as in the surrounding stanzas; translated accordingly. The four stanzas on loving-kindness, compassion, joy and equanimity correspond to the four immeasurables. The closing lines on Vajradhara are terse and their syntax is uncertain. Lāsyā, Mālā, Gītā, Nṛtyā and Puṣpā render the Tibetan names of the offering goddesses (sgeg mo, phreng ba, glu, gar ma, me tog) of the Vajradhātu maṇḍala. The lines on Bhṛkuṭi and on "making all the buddhas afraid" are translated as they stand, though their sense in this context is obscure. "The secret sūtra of the Great Yoga" may refer to a scripture of the Mahāyoga class rather than a generic description. The text reads thag khung, evidently a slip for thab khung, "hearth," and is translated so. The term sman ma, rendered "medicinal substance" in the stanzas on the bodhisattva, Vajrasattva and the Buddha, is uncertain; it may instead denote a female figure or effigy associated with each. "Consort" renders rig pa (vidyā) in its tantric sense of a female partner. The "royal sūtra of the Lord of Secrets" (mdo sde gsang dbang rgyal po) presumably refers to the scripture named in the chapter titles as the lord of secret sūtras; the identification is uncertain. "Bezoar" renders gi wang (gorocanā). In the line on the conduct of awakening the text has sbyod, read as spyod, "conduct." The closing verse naming the wide-eyed one and the vajra-eyed and wisdom-eyed goddesses is elliptical; chogs is read as tshogs, "host," and the identities of the figures are uncertain. The text reads sang pa chen po, evidently a slip for gsang ba chen po, "the great secret"; and rta babs bzhes, read as rta babs bzhis, "with four archways." In the list of the sixteen vajra deities, ngas byed is obscure and is rendered "the one who delights" on the assumption that it stands for dgyes byed (Vajrasādhu's position). The final instruction, gzhung 'di khyed kyis ci 'dir byin, is unclear; the interrogative ci may be a slip, and the line is taken as a permission to give the text to the fortunate. The litany of one hundred and eight names is a string of epithets of Vajrasattva in his wrathful aspect; many lines could be read either as names or as descriptions, and I have kept already established names (Vajraketu, Vajrayakṣa, Vajramuṣṭi, Vajrasādhu, Vajra Hook, Vajra Wrath, Vajra Descent, Vajra Joy) and rendered the rest descriptively. In "great noose, great power," དབང is taken as power or control rather than empowerment, as the context of noose and subjugation requires. The line ལྕགས་ཀྱུ་བསྡིགས་མེད is unclear; I read བསྡིགས་མེད as "without (fear of) threat," hence "hook beyond all threat." The final lines on the twelfth section (bam po) and the volume label are scribal and catalog notices, translated as such.
